(Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Many thanks to the authors of these wonderful resources!\\n\\n* [TwoPUtils](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils) 2P preprocessing code for the Giocomo lab, by Mark Plitt\\n* [Suite2p](https://github.com/MouseLand/suite2p) by Carsen Stringer and Marius Pachitariu\\n* [GLM code](https://github.com/sytseng/GLM_Tensorflow_2) by Shih-Yi Tseng\\n* [factorized k-means clustering](https://github.com/ahwillia/lvl) by Alex Williams\\n* [piecewise time warp model](https://github.com/ahwillia/affinewarp) by Alex Williams\\n* Circular-circular correlation code from [phase precession](https://github.com/CINPLA/phase-precession) by Richard Kempter\\n\\n\\n1. Git clone each of the above repos if you haven't already:\\n```bash\\ngit clone https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils.git\\ngit clone https://github.com/MouseLand/suite2p\\n```\\netc.\\n\\n\\n2. Activate your environment\\n```bash\\nconda activate <envname>\\n```\\n\\n3. Pip install at least TwoPUtils and suite2p as packages. If all the repos live in one parent directory, it would look like this:\\n```bash\\ncd Sosa_et_al_2024\\npip install -e .\\ncd ../TwoPUtils\\npip install -e .\\ncd ../suite2p\\npip install .\\ncd ../phase-precession\\npip install .\\n```\\nThe other repos will be added to your sys path in specific analyses, or you can pip install them if you prefer. \\n\\nWhen importing packages into your code, if you get an error like `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'PyQt5.sip'`, try the following:\\n```bash\\npip install pyqt5-sip\\npip install pyqt5\\n```\\n\\n## Path dictionary\\n\\nTo handle different data paths across experimenters (or users of this repo, once they download data to test), the current solution is to load experimenter-specific path dictionaries.\\n\\nSave-as `path_dict_example.py` with a new name, e.g. `path_dict_username.py`, and edit the file with the paths for your system. \\n\\nNote this file contains a path for a remote data server (called oak or GDrive here) (both were mounted on Mari's local machine).\\n\\nAt the top of your code, import the path dictionary:\\n```python\\nfrom reward_relative.path_dict_username import path_dictionary as path_dict\\n```\\n## Data download\\n\\nSee [these instructions](docs/data_download.md).\\n\\n## Data organization\\n\\nProcessed data (starting with `sess` classes) exist in 3 current levels of organiation:\\n1. `sess`: class that stores the fluorescence data synchronized with the VR data. In their rawest form, they do NOT contain dF/F (dFF).\\n   1. dFF and trial_matrices (spatially binned data) can be added separately via methods of the sess class\\n   2. See make_session_pkl.ipynb\\n   3. Original code to construct the sess lives in the TwoPUtils repo\\n2. `multi_anim_sess`: computes dFF, calculates place cells, adds details like a trial set dictionary, and collects these and the sess data for multiple animals on a single day. Useful so you can work off a constant set of place cell IDs (since place cells are identified by their spatial information relative to a shuffle, which is stochastic for each run of the shuffle for cells that are borderline significant).\\n   1. See the [multi_anim_sess README](docs/multi_anim_sess_README.md) for a detailed description.\\n   1. See also `./notebooks/make_multi_anim_sess.ipynb`\\n3. `dayData`: class that takes multi_anim_sess as an input and performs additional computations like finding place cell peaks, computing circular distance between peaks relative to reward, computing correlation matrices, etc.\\n   1. Original sess data are not re-saved here, but a copy of the trial matrices are kept.\\n   2. `dayData.py` lives in the reward_relative modules\\n   3. Use `./notebooks/make_multiDayData_class.ipynb` to generate this class and save it as a pickle.\\n\\n## Preprocessing guide\\n\\n[Order of operations for running preprocessing](docs/preprocessing_guide.md)\\n\\n### Using jupytext for .ipynb version control\\n\\nJupyter notebooks are great for debugging and plotting, but sharing jupyter notebooks or merging them across branches with git can be a mess (because of the outputs). \\n\\n[Jupytext](https://jupytext.readthedocs.io/en/latest/install.html) is a useful tool that we can use to synchronize jupyter notebooks to markdown (.md) files for vastly improved version control. Edits can now be easily compared and merged across branches, for instance, because notebook outputs and formatting are excluded or converted to plain text. This is also very useful if you are trying out my notebooks but having trouble loading the .ipynb in your IDE of choice -- the .md file will let you start fresh with the same code, but without any saved outputs or cell runs.\\n\\n1. To install jupytext in your virtual environment:\\n```\\npip install jupytext --upgrade\\n```\\n\\n2. To synchronize a .ipynb with a .md:\\n```\\njupytext --set-formats ipynb,md --sync notebook.ipynb\\n```\\n\\n3. If you come across a .md without a .ipynb or you want to start fresh from the .md, just synchronize in the other direction:\\n```\\njupytext --set-formats ipynb,md --sync notebook.md\\n```\\n\\n\\n## A note about stochasticity in exact results\\n\\nMany of our computations, including thresholds for classifying individual cells, rely on shuffles or other randomized functions. For instance, when determining which cells are place cells, we compare the spatial information of each cell to the distribution of spatial information scores within the animal resulting from 100 shuffles per cell of position relative to neural activity. Since the shuffle is randomly generated each time the code is run, there will always be +/- a few cells that pass as place cells/not place cells each time, for the cells that have borderline significant spatial information (consider the case where a cell has p=0.04 on one run of the shuffle and p=0.05 on another run). A similar type of shuffle criterion is used to define reward-relative cells. \\n\\nThis results in some stochasticity, such that if a user runs the whole pipeline from raw or early-level pre-processed data to plotting figures, they will not necessarily generate the __exact__ same plot that is in the paper. Importantly, however, the overall result should be the same, just perhaps with a slightly different n of included cells or slightly different p-value (but same order of magnitude) from the result reported in the paper, as the paper results came from a particular iteration of all of these randomized computations. We view this as a positive scientifically, because population-level results do not depend on the inclusion or exclusion of a handful of cells. \\n\\nTo replicate the plots exactly as they are in the paper, the post-processed version of the data used for the manuscript will be provided. \\n\"\n   328→          }\n   329→        ]\n   330→      },\n   331→      \"extra\": {\n   332→ seconds\\nProcess exited with code 0\\nOriginal token count: 1116\\nOutput:\\n# Preprocessing guide\\n\\nThis is meant to serve as a reference for how data were preprocessed from the raw imaging/VR files to the several levels of data structures used for analysis.\\n\\nAll file paths are written as if we are in the current repo, i.e. the pip installable modules will be listed as `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\nIf I say \\\"module\\\" I mean to look for a .py file in `./src/reward_relative`  \\\\\\n\\n### Directory structure\\n\\n* data_root: e.g. `/data/`\\n    * `2P_Data` (sometimes omitted locally)\\n        * animal\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyyy\\n                * scene: e.g. Env1_LocationA\\n                    * If you copied the scanbox files locally, they would be here as .sbx and .mat alongside the below directory\\n                    * session + scan directory: e.g. Env1_LocationA_002_001; session (index of Unity VR file) = 002, scan number (index of scan in Scanbox) = 001\\n                        * suite2p\\n                            * plane0 (plane1, combined, etc if multiplane)\\n    * `VR_Data`\\n        * animal\\n            * date: dd_mm_yyy\\n                * SQLite files: scene_session.sqlite, e.g. Env1_LocationA_2.sqlite\\n\\n### Order of operations:\\n\\n0. Update `./src/reward_relative/sessions_dict.py` with the metadata from each recording session, with one dictionary entry per animal in either `single_plane` or `multi_plane`\\n    1. Include date, scene name, session, scan number, experiment day (1-indexed)\\n1. Extract binaries, run suite2p to do motion correction and find ROIs\\n    1. In `./notebooks/suite2p_notebooks`, duplicate an existing notebook and rename for the current animal\\n    2. Manually edit with the sessions you want to process\\n    3. Manually edit any suite2p parameters you want to change (for example, if you think the expression was weak, you could lower threshold_scaling from 1 to 0.6-0.8)\\n    4. For Scanbox data, running the main notebook cell will convert the .sbx files to .h5 and then run suite2p\\n2. Curate cells in the suite2p GUI\\n    1. `iscell.npy` is automatically updated and saved during curation\\n    2. Be sure to backup curated data so you don't lose all that work!\\n3. Copy VR_Data to the local machine with rclone or similar\\n4. Make `sess` class which aligns imaging data to Unity VR data.\\n    1. The code for the class object itself is in a separate repo, `TwoPUtils` \\n    2. The function that actually interpolates/aligns VR data to the slower imaging frame rate is `vr_align_to_2P` in `TwoPUtils/preprocessing.py`\\n    3. Use `./notebooks/make_session_pkl.ipynb` to make sess for each animal and list of experiment days that you want.\\n    4. `sess` pickles will be saved in `/data_root/sess/`\\n    5. Note `sess` does not contain deltaF/F -- this happens next, because it is more customizable. The idea is that you should only ever have to make the `sess` class once.\\n5. VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal\\u2019s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day\\u20091 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450\\u2009cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50\\u2009cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day\\u20093, the reward zone was moved within a \\u2018switch\\u2019 session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day\\u20095, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day\\u20097. On day\\u20098, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On \\u2018stay\\u2019 days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 group of mice (n\\u2009=\\u20093) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray \\u2018teleport\\u2019 zone (~50\\u2009cm\\u2009+\\u2009temporal jitter up to 10\\u2009s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\\n9:Mice were pre-trained on a \\u2018running\\u2019 task on a 350\\u2009cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50\\u2009cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25\\u2009cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20\\u2009s increased the inter-reward distance by 10\\u2009cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30\\u2009s, the distance decreased by 10\\u2009cm, with a maximum reward location of 300\\u2009cm. Upon consistent completion of 300\\u2009cm distance in under 20\\u2009s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray \\u2018teleport zone\\u2019 (50\\u2009cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30\\u201340\\u2009min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., 5.8\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20091.9\\u2009days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\\n11:## Hidden reward zone task\\n13:The main \\u2018switch\\u2019 task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450\\u2009cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a \\u2018hidden\\u2019, unmarked 50\\u2009cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80\\u2013130\\u2009cm; zone B, 200\\u2013250\\u2009cm; zone C, 320\\u2013370\\u2009cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a \\u2018teleport period\\u2019 that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (\\u2018jitter period\\u2019) between 1 and 5\\u2009s (5\\u201310\\u2009s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50\\u2009cm giving the appearance of a gray \\u2018tunnel,\\u2019 before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0\\u2009cm.\\n15:Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n\\u2009=\\u20099 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days\\u20091\\u20132 of the task. On day\\u20093 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a\\u2013c). The new reward zone was maintained on day\\u20094. On day\\u20095 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day\\u20096 and moved back to the original location on day\\u20097 (for example, A). On day\\u20098, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14\\u2009days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\\n17:An additional \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 cohort (n\\u2009=\\u20093 mice) experienced only ENV 1 and one fixed reward location throughout the 14\\u2009days (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f).\\n19:We targeted 80\\u2013100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., 80.5\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20097.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50\\u2009min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 \\u2018warm-up\\u2019 trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day\\u20091 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\\n23:We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920\\u2009nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica \\u00d725, 1.0\\u2009NA, 2.6\\u2009mm working distance), ranged from 15\\u201368\\u2009mW for mice m2\\u2013m7, 50\\u2013100\\u2009mW for m10 and m11, and 13\\u201340\\u2009mW for m12\\u201319. The task was imaged starting from day\\u20091 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day\\u20093 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11\\u2013m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15\\u2013m19 on day\\u20091 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5\\u2009Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090.7\\u2009mm FOV (512\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27\\u2009\\u00b5m, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31\\u2009Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5\\u2009Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the \\u2018searchref\\u2019 plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\\n27:The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155\\u20132172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\\n29:To compute the \\u0394F/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20\\u2009s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129\\u2009s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal\\u2019s running speed, excluding 0.42\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.85% of cells (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. across mice and days).\\n33:To avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro\\u2013Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. In the main text, percentages of cells classified by remapping type are reported as mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. out of all place cells identified on the specified days. Cells were excluded from certain analyses only if they did not meet pre-established criteria as \\u2018place cells\\u2019 (defined in \\u2018Place cell identification\\u2019), to ensure that included cells provided reliable information about position in the task. Averaged data in figures are shown as mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.e.m. unless otherwise indicated. For the distributions of sequence positions over days in Fig. 4 and Extended Data Fig. 4, s.e.m. shading is omitted for clarity, but the variance for these distributions is accounted for in linear mixed-effects models to quantify these plots (see \\u2018Linear mixed-effects models\\u2019). For data displayed as violin plots, the shape is computed as a kernel density estimate to the bounds of the data, the solid center line marks the median and additional horizontal dashed lines mark the inner quartile.\\n37:The number of mice to include was determined by having coverage of every possible reward sequence permutation (six sequences) with at least one mouse in the switch task. In addition, our mouse sample sizes were similar to those reported in previous publications. Mice were randomly selected to experience the switch task (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice) versus the \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 task in which the reward zone was not moved (n\\u2009=\\u20093).\\n41:AP and ML ROI coordinates were computed as the 2D center of mass of ROI pixels, relative to each FOV. In multi-plane imaged animals, ROIs were separated according to their deep or superficial imaging plane. To estimate deep-superficial (dorso-ventral (DV)) coordinates in single-plane animals, we acquired a z-stack scan after the day\\u200914 task session that extended \\u2212100\\u2009\\u00b5m below (\\u221260\\u2009\\u00b5m below in one mouse) to +100\\u2009\\u00b5m above the FOV in the DV axis, in 2\\u2009\\u00b5m steps with ~50 frames per step. Each step was independently motion-corrected as described above, and the mean of each step was used to compile the 3D z-stack array. We then took the AP projection of each ML slice of the z-stack (that is, looking at the side view of the z-stack). We minimum-filtered (4\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090 pixel window, ML\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009DV) and smoothed (2\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20092 pixel s.d. Gaussian) each slice, then detected the most prominent dip in brightness in the DV axis to approximate the location of cell bodies, under the assumption that the GCaMP signal is typically absent in the nuclei and brighter in the surrounding neuropil. This yielded a per-pixel estimate of the depth of the pyramidal layer center from the dorsal surface of the z-stack, which we then smoothed (40\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200940 pixel s.d. Gaussian) to approximate the cell layer \\u2018curvature\\u2019 across the FOV. The curvature estimate was registered to the mean FOV of each imaging session. Given that the imaging plane transects this curvature, calculating the mean DV distance of each ROI (across pixels) from the curvature provides an estimate of where each ROI resides in the deep-to-superficial axis.\\n45:The capacitive lick sensor allowed us to detect single licks. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were removed from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n\\u2009=\\u200981 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). These trials were detected by >30% of the 0.0645\\u2009s imaging frame samples in the trial containing a cumulative lick count >2, as this would have produced a sustained lick rate \\u226520\\u2009Hz. Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10\\u2009cm bins), then divided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. We quantified licking precision over blocks of ten trials using an anticipatory lick ratio, computed as:\\n49:where Lickin is the mean lick rate in a 50\\u2009cm \\u2018anticipatory\\u2019 zone before the start of the reward zone and Lickout is the mean rate outside of this zone and the reward zone. The reward zone itself is thus excluded to exclude consummatory licks (Extended Data Fig. 1e). A ratio of 1 indicates perfect licking only in the anticipatory zone, a ratio of \\u22121 indicates licking only outside of this zone and a ratio of 0 indicates chance licking everywhere, excluding the reward zone.\\n53:For all neural spatial activity analyses, we excluded activity when the animal was moving at <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121. To obtain an activity matrix of trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009position bins for each cell, we binned the 450\\u2009cm linear track into 45 bins of 10\\u2009cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each position bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\\n55:We defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\n59:where pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian.\\n63:To identify spatial peak firing of place cells, we used the position bin of the maximum unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F, as this signal is the closest to the raw data, averaged across trials within a set (pre-switch or post-switch). There was no restriction to field boundaries, thus allowing cells to have multiple fields with a single identified peak.\\n65:# Trial-by-trial similarity matrices\\n67:Correlation matrices were computed using the spatially binned deconvolved activity on each trial, smoothed with a 20\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for single cells and a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for population vectors. This resulted in a matrix, A, of j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins per cell. For population vectors, single-cell matrices were horizontally concatenated such that A was j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n cells. Each trial was z-scored across the position axis. For single cells, the trial-by-trial correlation matrix C was computed as:\\n73:We defined the cell remapping types shown in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2 as follows: TR, significant SI before and after the switch, with spatial peaks (see \\u2018Spatial peak firing identification\\u2019) \\u226450\\u2009cm from each other before versus after; disappearing, significant SI before but not after the switch, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is less than the 50th percentile of the per-trial mean dF/F before; appearing, significant SI after but not before, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is greater than 1\\u2009s.d. above the mean in trials before (appearing and disappearing cells that had sufficiently reliable activity despite their rate-remapping to be captured by the RR criteria below were removed from the appearing and disappearing groups); remap near reward, significant SI before and after, with spatial peaks \\u226450\\u2009cm from the starts of both reward zones; and remap far from reward, significant SI before and after, not TR, with peaks >50\\u2009cm from the start of at least one reward zone.\\n79:For stringent statistics on putative RR remapping (Fig. 2c\\u2013i and Extended Data Fig. 2g\\u2013n), we restricted the set of included place cells to require significant SI in the pre-switch and post-switch trial sets in each session. We converted the position timeseries of the animal to periodic coordinates, setting 0\\u2009cm on the track to \\u2212\\u03c0 and 450\\u2009cm to \\u03c0. Spatial peaks for each cell were re-computed using binned dF/F at a periodic bin size of 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945 (corresponding to ~10\\u2009cm). Putative TR cells were removed by excluding cells with spatial peaks within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after the switch. The periodic coordinates were then circularly rotated to align the start of each reward zone at 0, to measure the signed circular distance of spatial peaks relative to the start of each reward zone. For scatter plots of spatial peaks in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2, points are jittered by a random amount between \\u2212\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 and +\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 for visualization only. We measured the circularly wrapped difference between relative peaks after minus before the switch, creating a distribution that can be thought of as orthogonal to the unity line in Fig. 2d,f. We compared this distribution to a \\u2018random-remapping\\u2019 shuffle, generated by maintaining the pre-switch peak of each cell and circularly permuting its post-switch firing 1,000 times by a random offset of 0\\u201344 bins. To define a candidate range of RR remapping variability, we computed the circular difference between the maximum and minimum bin of the distribution of true differences between RR peaks that exceeded the upper 95% of the shuffle, divided by two and averaged across switches. In an initial cohort of n\\u2009=\\u20097 mice, this produced a mean range of \\u00b10.656 radians (~46.9\\u2009cm) around 0, which is captured by a 50\\u2009cm threshold given our 10\\u2009cm bin size. This threshold was subsequently used to identify candidate RR remapping cells (see below).\\n83:As we observed variable remapping dynamics over trials after a reward switch (Fig. 2a,b and Extended Data Fig. 2a), which could affect SI scores, we relaxed the SI criterion for RR cells such that they were required to have significant SI either before or after the switch but not necessarily both. We next implemented two criteria to identify a robust subpopulation of RR cells. First, we identified place cells with peak spatial firing relative to the reward zone start within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after a reward switch (within the orange candidate zone highlighted in Fig. 2d,f), allowing for some variability in RR precision. These cells could thus include \\u2018remap near reward\\u2019 and \\u2018remap far from reward\\u2019 cells (see \\u2018Remapping category definitions\\u2019). Second, to reduce the influence of noise in spatial peak detection and account for the shape of the firing fields, we cross-correlated the cells\\u2019 trial-averaged, spatially binned dF/F aligned to the reward zone pre-switch versus post-switch. We predicted that RR cells should have a peak cross-correlation close to zero on this reward-centered axis. We calculated a shuffle for the cross-correlation by circularly permuting the activity in each post-switch trial by a random offset between 1 and 45 bins 500 times, requiring that the real cross-correlogram had a peak exceeding the upper 97.5% confidence interval of the shuffle and that the offset of this peak was within five bins (~50\\u2009cm) of zero. This approach provides a metric for how stable each cell\\u2019s firing is relative to reward, identifying a subpopulation of cells whose fields were maximally Pearson-correlated in periodic coordinates relative to reward, in contrast to the TR cells, which were maximally correlated relative to the original linear track coordinates (Extended Data Fig. 2o\\u2013q). Place cells that passed both criteria were defined as RR.\\n85:## Relationship between distance run in the teleport zone and trial-to-trial variability\\n87:We measured the distance run in the teleport zone at the end of each trial by integrating the animal\\u2019s speed from the first frame in the teleport period to the last frame before re-entry to the start of the track. We then computed the trial-wise spatial peak error for each RR cell as the difference between the cell\\u2019s spatial peak location on each trial and its trial-averaged spatial peak location within either the trials before or after the reward switch (circular error converted back to cm). We next performed a Spearman correlation between the distance run during the teleport and the error on the following trial (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f).\\n93:Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, candidate place fields were identified as regions of \\u226520\\u2009cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where \\u2018significantly active\\u2019 was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1\\u2009s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\\n100:To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field \\u00b110\\u2009cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1\\u2009s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field\\u2019s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field\\u2019s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\\n104:We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular\\u2013linear regression38 to predict the animal\\u2019s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model \\u2018decode score\\u2019 at each timepoint t is given by \\\\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal\\u2019s true RR position and \\\\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945, ~10\\u2009cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\\n108:For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F in circular track coordinates (not aligned to reward, although aligning to reward makes no difference for the circular sequence order). We sorted neurons by their peak firing positions from activity averaged over the odd trials before the switch. This sort was cross-validated by plotting the trial-averaged activity of the even trials before the switch (for example, left-hand columns of Fig. 4a, normalized to the mean activity of each cell within a session), then applying this sort to the trials after the switch. The sequence positions before versus after were then taken as the peak firing positions of the trial-averaged \\u2018even-before\\u2019 trials and the trial-averaged \\u2018after\\u2019 trials. Preservation of sequence order was quantified as the circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions before versus after. Although a P\\u2009value can be directly computed for this correlation coefficient, its significance was further validated by randomly permuting the cell identities of the neurons after the switch and re-computing the correlation coefficient 1,000 times to obtain a null distribution. The observed correlation coefficient, rho, was considered significant if it was outside 95% of the null distribution using a two-tailed test. The P value was calculated as:\\n114:For sequences followed across days, we again used the cross-validated sort from trials before the switch (or the first half of trials if reward did not move) on the reference day (the first day in each day pair) and applied this sort to trials after the switch (or second half) on both the reference day and all trials on the target day (the second day in the pair). To measure drift between days, we computed the across-day circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions on reference day \\u2018after\\u2019 trials versus target day \\u2018before\\u2019 trials. A minimum of five followed cells in the sequence was required to compute a correlation coefficient.\\n116:To quantify the density of sequences, we divided the number of cells with peaks in each 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945 position bin by the total number of place cells for that animal. The fixed effect was usually either the switch index or day-pair index as a continuous variable unless otherwise noted. Random effects were mouse identity. In cases of only one fixed effect term, no corrections for multiple comparisons were made. Random intercepts for each mouse were allowed; we also confirmed that including random slopes did not affect the results. When the dependent variable was reported in fractions of cells, we applied a logit transform to the fractions when a large proportion of the values were near zero. In these cases, an expit transform was applied to the model prediction to plot it with the fractional data points. In most cases, however, we chose to use the original fractions in the model for interpretability, and we confirmed that performing a logit transform on the fractions did not qualitatively change the results and only modestly changed the model fit.\\n122:# Analysis of teleport periods\\n124:In the sessions for which we imaged the whole teleport period (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), we re-identified place cells including activity during the \\u2018tunnel\\u2019 and part of the \\u2018jitter\\u2019 in the teleport period (see \\u2018Behavioral training and VR tasks\\u2019 for description), and re-categorized remapping types as either TR or RR from this set of place cells. To simulate the \\u2018ideal\\u2019 RR remapping destination if RR fields moved by the exact amount that the reward zone moved, we added the circular difference between reward zone starts to the pre-switch peak activity position of each RR cell.\\n126:# Analysis of rewarded versus omission trials\\n128:Analyses were restricted to trial sets (before or after the reward switch) that had at least three omission trials within the set.\\n132:We first fit five different time warp model types\\u2014shift, linear, piecewise one knot, piecewise two knots and piecewise three knots36\\u2014on the matrix of speed profiles within a trial set (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20091, expanded in the third dimension for compatibility with the time warp algorithm). These models apply warping functions of increasing nonlinear complexity to stretch and compress the data on each trial for maximal alignment. We included both rewarded and omission trials in the fitting procedure to find the best alignment across them. Model fit was assessed using the mean squared error between the time-warped speed profile of each trial and the mean across time-warped trials (Extended Data Fig. 8a). The piecewise three knots model most often produced the best fit (lowest mean squared error) across sessions (Extended Data Fig. 8c). We therefore re-fit all sessions with piecewise three knots to ensure that model selection could not influence the results. We then applied the model transform to the deconvolved neural activity matrix for the same set of trials (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons; excluding neural data at movement speeds of <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121). For neural analysis in Fig. 6, we then focused on trial sets before the switch in which the reward zone was at position \\u2018A\\u2019 or \\u2018B\\u2019, because the mean rewarded versus omission speed profiles were more similar on these trials than after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 8f) or when the reward was at \\u2018C\\u2019 (as there is less room at the end of the track for running speed to reach its maximum from position \\u2018C\\u2019 on omission trials; see Extended Data Figs. 1 and 8h for examples; see Extended Data Fig. 8 for analyses of trials after the switch and for all reward zones).\\n134:## Reward versus omission index\\n136:We compared neural activity on rewarded versus omission trials for RR cells with spatial peaks between the start of the reward zone and the end of the track (under the hypothesis that these cells could receive information about whether reward was received or not on that trial). Reward versus omission indices of 1 indicate an exclusive firing preference for rewarded trials, \\u22121 indicates an exclusive preference for omission trials and 0 indicates equal firing between rewards and omissions. We confirmed that the reward versus omission index calculated from the model-transformed activity was highly correlated with the reward versus omission index calculated from the original activity (Extended Data Fig. 8e). Using a linear mixed-effects model (Extended Data Fig. 8j), we found that there was no significant change in the median reward versus omission index for each animal across days as a result of the model fit or as a result of the original mean squared error between rewarded and omission trials. We therefore combined neurons across days for presentation in Fig. 6e.\\n144:We implemented a Poisson GLM developed previously37 to predict the deconvolved calcium event time series of individual neurons from a set of task and movement variables. Each movement variable was separately quantile-transformed to encode the distribution of movement dynamics similarly across animals. The quantiles were expanded with B-spline basis functions using the SplineTranformer method of Python\\u2019s sklearn package, with three polynomial degrees and five knots, resulting in seven bases (5\\u2009+\\u20093\\u2009\\u2212\\u20091) per movement variable. Individual sessions (including trials both before and after the reward switch) were split into 85% training trials and 15% testing trials, using the GroupShuffleSplit method of Python\\u2019s sklearn package with a consistent random seed. Specifically, trials were allocated such that the training trials included 85% of rewarded trials before the switch, 85% of rewarded trials after the switch and 85% of all omission trials. The training trials were further divided for fivefold cross-validation during the fitting procedure. We first scaled activity between 0 and 1 to be comparable across neurons, normalizing each neuron to itself by subtracting its minimum and dividing by the difference between maximum and minimum activity over the session. We then created a population vector for each subpopulation of interest (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons), used to create the population correlation matrices shown in Fig. 8 and Extended Data Fig. 10 (see \\u2018Trial-by-trial similarity matrices\\u2019). This model factorizes the normalized population activity array Xijn as:\\n178:where {U}_{i}^{\\\\,(k)} is an I\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009K matrix in which every row is a trial i and each column is a one-hot vector encoding the assignment of the trial to each cluster k. {V}_{{jn}}^{\\\\,(k)} is a K\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009J\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009N array specifying the J\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009N (bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009simultaneously imaged neurons) cluster centroid, or population \\u2018map\\u2019, for each cluster k.\\n180:To determine whether the k\\u2009=\\u20092 model fit was significantly better than could be expected from a session with only one \\u2018map\\u2019, we first cross-validated the model performance using a randomized speckled holdout of 10% of the data over 50 repetitions, then compared the uncentered test R2 to the test performance on a shuffled dataset. Sessions in which the real model\\u2019s performance at k\\u2009=\\u20092 clusters exceeded that of the shuffle (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test, P\\u2009<\\u20090.05) were accepted for further analysis (n\\u2009=\\u200950 out of 77 sessions for the RR population vector). The distance score P on each trial i is computed as:\\n186:When Pi\\u2009=\\u20090, the network activity is at the midpoint between clusters, but there are no restrictions on how often Pi can cross this midpoint. To identify an inflection point between sets of trials for which the network activity transitioned from being primarily in cluster one to primarily in cluster two, we fit a sigmoid to the distance score and defined the inflection point of the sigmoid as the \\u2018remap trial\\u2019. This remap trial can be thought of as the point at which the network activity significantly deviates from its pre-switch map.\\n188:To identify transitions in behavior, we similarly maximum-normalized the spatially binned lick counts or spatially binned running speed, yielding matrices of i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20091. correlated with running speed)\\n    or ratio of 99th prctile to mean spatially-binned dF/F\\n    values (captures cells with very high mean dF/F).\\n\\n    :param sess: session class\\n    :param trial_mat_prop.append(np.percentile(_trial_mat[~nanmask], 75))\\n\\n            trial_mat_max = np.percentile(_trial_mat[~nanmask], 99)\\n\\n            ratio = trial_mat_max / np.mean(_trial_mat[~nanmask])\\n            trial_mat_ratio.append(ratio)\\n\\n        is_int = trial_mat_ratio < np.percentile(trial_mat_ratio, prct)\\n\\n    elif method == 'speed':\\n Each trial spans from its start frame to its teleport frame (inclusive, i.e., `stop + 1` for Python slicing).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_trial_slices(trial_start_signal: np.ndarray, teleport_signal: np.ndarray) -> list[tuple[int, int]]:\n    starts = np.flatnonzero(trial_start_signal > 0.5)\n    teleports = np.flatnonzero(teleport_signal > 0.5)\n    ...\n    n = min(starts.size, teleports.size)\n    slices: list[tuple[int, int]] = []\n    for start, stop in zip(starts[:n], teleports[:n]):\n        if stop < start:\n            continue\n        slices.append((int(start), int(stop) + 1))\n```\n\niii. The AI documented: \"Reconstruct trial start/end indices from trial_start and teleport signals in NWB. This matches the reference trial definition more closely than relying only on trial-number changes.\"\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered based on lick artifact detection only. A trial is excluded if more than 35% of its imaging frames have a cumulative lick count > 2. This threshold (0.35) matches the reference code's `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data`. The AI does NOT apply speed filtering (excluding timepoints with speed < 2 cm/s) which the reference code does.\n\nii.\n```python\nLICK_ARTIFACT_FRACTION = 0.35\n\nlick_trial = arrays.lick[start:stop_exclusive]\nif lick_trial.size and np.mean(lick_trial > 2) > LICK_ARTIFACT_FRACTION:\n    lick_artifact[trial_id] = True\n...\nfor trial_id, (start, stop_exclusive) in enumerate(trial_slices):\n    if lick_artifact[trial_id]:\n        continue\n```\n\niii. The AI noted: \"Matches glmUtils.get_timeseries_data in the reference code\" and reported 69/12,216 trials removed (0.56%), close to the paper's 81/12,376 (0.65%). The paper text mentions \">30%\" threshold but the reference code uses 0.35; the AI followed the code.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `processing/ophys/Deconvolved/plane0/data` in the NWB files, which contains pre-computed deconvolved calcium activity (events). This is filtered by `iscell` from `processing/ophys/ImageSegmentation/PlaneSegmentation`.\n\nii.\n```python\nneural_group = f[\"processing/ophys/Deconvolved/plane0\"]\nneural = neural_group[\"data\"][()].astype(np.float32, copy=False)\nroi_ids = neural_group[\"rois\"][()].astype(np.int64, copy=False)\niscell = seg[\"iscell\"][()][roi_ids, 0] > 0.5\nneural = neural[:, iscell]\n```\n\niii. The AI justified: \"Use NWB Deconvolved as the neural signal: The paper's decoder and SI analyses operate on deconvolved activity after dF/F, and this stream is already provided in the archive.\"\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The neural data undergoes the following processing: (1) Filter to curated cells using `iscell` on ROI indices referenced by the response matrix. (2) Crop to the common minimum length of neural and behavioral frames. (3) For 31 Hz sessions (m17, m18), rebin by factor 2 (summing pairs of frames) to achieve the common 15.5078125 Hz rate. (4) Extract per-trial slices and transpose to (neurons, time).\n\nii.\n```python\n# Cell filtering\niscell = seg[\"iscell\"][()][roi_ids, 0] > 0.5\nneural = neural[:, iscell]\n\n# Common length\nt_common = min(t_neural, t_behavior)\n\n# Per-trial extraction and rebinning\nneural_trial = arrays.neural[start:stop_exclusive].T.astype(np.float32, copy=False)\nif factor == 2:\n    neural_trial = rebin_2d_sum_time_last(neural_trial, factor)\n```\n\niii. The AI noted: \"Restricted neural loading to ROI indices actually referenced by the response matrix and then to curated iscell ROIs.\" The rebinning uses summation for neural data (consistent with deconvolved event counts).\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Neural data is filtered at the cell level by the suite2p `iscell` flag (manual curation). Only ROIs referenced by the `Deconvolved/plane0/rois` index are considered, and among those, only cells with `iscell[:, 0] > 0.5` are kept. The AI did NOT implement the additional putative interneuron exclusion based on dF/F-speed correlation > 0.5 described in the paper.\n\nii.\n```python\nroi_ids = neural_group[\"rois\"][()].astype(np.int64, copy=False)\niscell = seg[\"iscell\"][()][roi_ids, 0] > 0.5\nneural = neural[:, iscell]\nroi_ids = roi_ids[iscell]\n```\n\niii. The AI acknowledged the missing interneuron filter: \"exact dF/F-speed-correlation interneuron exclusion was not re-run because the archive exposes deconvolved responses but not the exact reference dF/F stream.\" The AI noted this affected only 0.42 +/- 0.85% of cells.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to trial start. Each trial's neural data begins at the `trial_start` frame and ends at the `teleport` frame (inclusive). The first time bin of each trial corresponds to the trial start frame, with `off_start = 0.0`.\n\nii.\n```python\nneural_trial = arrays.neural[start:stop_exclusive].T.astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\nWhere `start` comes from `np.flatnonzero(trial_start_signal > 0.5)`.\n\niii. Metadata records `temporal_alignment_event: \"trial_start\"` and `off_start: 0.0`.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The target temporal resolution is 1/15.5078125 Hz = ~64.5 ms. Sessions recorded at 15.5078125 Hz are used at native resolution. Sessions recorded at 31.015625 Hz (m17, m18) are rebinned by a factor of 2 to achieve the common rate.\n\nii.\n```python\nTARGET_RATE_HZ = 15.5078125\nTARGET_DT_S = 1.0 / TARGET_RATE_HZ\n\nif math.isclose(arrays.rate_hz, TARGET_RATE_HZ):\n    factor = 1\nelif math.isclose(arrays.rate_hz, 2.0 * TARGET_RATE_HZ):\n    factor = 2\n```\n\niii. The AI noted: \"Standardize all sessions to a common 15.5078125 Hz bin size: This is the dominant dataset rate and the paper's effective per-plane sampling rate.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* derived from?\n\ni. Time from start of trial is computed synthetically from the bin index and the target sampling rate. It is not read directly from any raw data variable.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_start = (np.arange(t_bins, dtype=np.float32) * TARGET_DT_S)\n```\n\niii. This creates a time vector starting at 0.0 with increments of `1/15.5078125` seconds per bin, representing elapsed time since trial start.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds*?\n\ni. Simple arithmetic: bin index multiplied by the time bin duration (1/15.5078125 s). No additional processing.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_from_start = (np.arange(t_bins, dtype=np.float32) * TARGET_DT_S)\n```\n\niii. Straightforward computation aligned with the trial-start alignment event.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It shares the same number of time bins (`t_bins = neural_trial.shape[1]`), so each time value corresponds to exactly one neural time bin. The first bin is time 0 (trial start).\n\nii.\n```python\nt_bins = neural_trial.shape[1]\ntime_from_start = (np.arange(t_bins, dtype=np.float32) * TARGET_DT_S)\n```\n\niii. Alignment is guaranteed by construction since both share the same number of bins.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment type* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `processing/behavior/BehavioralTimeSeries/environment/data` in the NWB file.\n\nii.\n```python\nenvironment=beh[\"environment/data\"][()][:t_common].astype(np.float32, copy=False),\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that environment values in the NWB are -1, 0, or 1, where -1 indicates invalid periods.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment type*?\n\ni. For each trial, the modal valid environment value (excluding negative values) is computed and broadcast across all time bins. Environment 0 maps to ENV1, environment 1 maps to ENV2.\n\nii.\n```python\nenv = arrays.environment[start:stop_exclusive]\nenv = env[env >= 0]\ntrial_env[trial_id] = mode_int(env, default=0)\n...\nenv_series = np.full((t_bins,), float(trial_env[trial_id]), dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The trial number is the within-session trial index (0-indexed), derived from the trial's position in the reconstructed trial list, not from the NWB `trial number` field.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_series = np.full((t_bins,), float(trial_id), dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii.
